Integers representable as a difference of two rational fourth powers
Ashleigh Ratcliffe, Nguyen Xuan Tho

Abstract
. In Section 6.6 of the book Number Theory, Volume I: Tools and Diophantine Equations, Graduate Texts in Mathematics, Volume 239, Springer (2007) , Cohen investigated the solubility of the equation n = x 4 + y 4 n=x^{4}+y^{4} in rational numbers x , y x,y for all positive integers n ≤ 10000 n\leq 10000 . Motivated by this, we study the equation n = x 4 − y 4 n=x^{4}-y^{4} and obtain the complete list of positive integers n ≤ 10000 n\leq 10000 that can be represented in this form for some nonzero rational numbers x x and y y .



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
14G05, 11D25, 11D41, 11Y50

1.
Introduction

The only case of Fermat’s Last Theorem proved by Fermat himself is the case
n=4
, which states that the equation
x^{4}+y^{4}=z^{4}
has no solutions in nonzero integers
x,y,z
. By rewriting this equation as
(x/z)^{4}+(y/z)^{4}=1
or
(z/y)^{4}-(x/y)^{4}=1
, we conclude that
1
cannot be expressed as either a sum or a difference of two nonzero rational fourth powers. It is therefore natural to ask which integers
n
admit such representations.

The question about the sum
x^{4}+y^{4}
has been well studied. Demjanenko
[
5
]
investigated the solubility of

(1)

x^{4}+y^{4}=n

in nonzero rational numbers
x,y
for positive integers
n
and proved that if the curve
x^{4}+y^{2}=n
has the rank at most
1
, then equation (
1
) has no solutions. Serre
[
11
]
investigated equation (
1
) for
n\leq 100
and gave the challenge of proving that all rational solutions to the equation
x^{4}+y^{4}=17
are
(x,y)=(\pm 2,\pm 1),(\pm 1,\pm 2)
. This challenge was later resolved by Flynn and Wetherell
[
6
]
. Bremner
[
3
]
and Silverman
[
13
]
extended Demjanenko’s work in different directions. Motivated by the work of Demjanenko
[
5
]
and Serre
[
11
]
, Cohen
[
4
, Section 6.6]
investigated equation (
1
) and determined its solubility in rational numbers
x,y
for all positive integers
n\leq 10000
, except for
n=7537
and
n=8882
. Bremner and Tho
[
2
]
completed these remaining cases, thereby finishing the list of all positive integers
n\leq 10000
representable as the sum of two rational fourth powers.

Cohen
[
4
, Chapter 6, Exercise 53]
asked to determine all positive integers
n\leq 100
for which the equation

(2)

x^{4}-y^{4}=n

is solvable in rational numbers
x,y
. This was done by Grechuk
[
7
, Section 6.3.4]
, where the problem was in fact solved for all
n\leq 218
, and the case
n=219
was left open. This case and the case
n=8575
were resolved by Tho in
[
14
]
. Here, we combine the methods of Grechuk
[
7
, Section 6.3.4]
and Tho
[
14
]
to obtain the complete list of positive integers
n\leq 10000
which can be represented as a difference of two nonzero rational fourth powers
1
1

1

One may add perfect fourth powers to our list to obtain the list of all positive integers
n\leq 10000
which can be represented as a difference of any (not necessarily nonzero) two rational fourth powers.
. This is on par with the survey of Cohen
[
4
]
and the work of Bremner and Tho
[
2
]
for the sum of two rational fourth powers. For the related problem of sum of two sixth powers, see the work of Newton and Rouse
[
9
]
. We now state the main result of this paper.

Theorem 1
.

The only integers
1\leq n\leq 10000
which can be represented as a difference of two nonzero rational fourth powers are precisely the numbers listed in Table
3
.

In Section
2
, we explain and implement the methods used in
[
7
]
, which solve (
2
) for most values of
n
in the range
1\leq n\leq 10000
. In Section
3
, we explain and implement the method from
[
14
]
to prove the insolubility of the remaining cases. For each value of
n
, the methods in this paper either prove that there are no rational solutions to (
2
), or produce a rational solution. The values of
n\leq 10000
for which equation (
2
) has a rational solution are listed in Table
3
.

An integer
n>0
is expressible as the difference of two nonzero rational fourth powers if and only if the equation

(3)

x^{4}-y^{4}=nz^{4}

has an integer solution
(x,y,z)
with
xyz\neq 0
.

If
n=k^{4}n^{\prime}
for some integer
k\geq 1
and
n^{\prime}
fourth-power free, then
(x,y)
is a rational solution to (
2
) if and only if
(x/k,y/k)
is a rational solution to
x^{4}-y^{4}=n^{\prime}
. Hence, it suffices to determine the solubility of (
2
) and (
3
) for fourth power free
n
.

2.
Elliptic Curves, Mordell-Weil sieve, and Pythagorean triples

2.1.
Easy reductions

Finding small integer solutions to (
3
) is easy with computer algebra systems such as Magma
[
1
]
. For example, given
n
, the code:

⬇

P
<
x
,
y
,
z
>

:=

ProjectiveSpace
(
Rationals
(),2);

C

:=

Curve
(
P
,
x
^4-
y
^4-
n

z
^4);

RationalPoints
(
C

:

Bound

:=

10000);

returns all solutions to (
3
) with height bounded by
10000
. This is how solutions in Table
3
were found. The difficult part is to prove that for the remaining values of
n
equation (
3
) has no integer solution with
xyz\neq 0
. Several known methods can be implemented to prove nonexistence of solutions.
First, for any nontrivial integer solution
(x,y,z)
to (
3
):

(X,Y)=\left(\dfrac{y^{2}}{z^{2}},\dfrac{x^{2}y}{z^{3}}\right)
is a rational solution to the equation
Y^{2}=X^{3}+nX
,

(X,Y)=\left(\dfrac{x^{2}}{z^{2}},\dfrac{xy^{2}}{z^{3}}\right)
is a rational solution to the equation
Y^{2}=X^{3}-nX
,

(X,Y)=\left(\dfrac{nx^{2}}{y^{2}},\dfrac{n^{2}xz^{2}}{y^{3}}\right)
is a rational solution to the equation
Y^{2}=X^{3}-n^{2}X
.

Hence, if we can prove that any of the curves

(4)

Y^{2}=X^{3}-n^{2}X,\quad Y^{2}=X^{3}-nX,\quad Y^{2}=X^{3}+nX

has no rational points corresponding to
xyz\neq 0
, then equation (
3
) has no nontrivial integer solutions. If any of the curves (
4
) have rank
0
, then it has finitely many rational points and these are its torsion points. We can use Magma
[
1
]
to find all torsion points of a given elliptic curve, and then we can easily check whether these points correspond to integer solutions
(x,y,z)
with
xyz\neq 0
of (
3
). This approach allows us to prove the insolubility of (
3
) for all integers
1\leq n\leq 10000
for which one of the curves (
4
) has rank
0
. From now on, we consider only values of
n
for which all the curves (
4
) have positive rank. For these values of
n
, we use the following theorem.

Theorem 2
.

[
7
, Section 6.3.4]

We can reduce the problem of solving (
3
) in integers to solving equations of the form

(5)

a^{2}u^{8}+b^{2}v^{8}=cw^{4}

for pairwise coprime positive integers
a,b,c
with
abc\in\{n/8,2n\}
and integer variables
u,v,w
such that

(6)

\gcd(au,bv)=\gcd(au,cw)=\gcd(bv,cw)=1.

Proof.

To be self-contained, we include the proof. Assume that an integer solution
(x,y,z)
with
xyz\neq 0
to (
3
) exists. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
x,y,z>0
, since if
(x,y,z)
is a solution, then so are
(\pm x,\pm y,\pm z)
. We may further assume that
\gcd(x,y,z)=1
, as if
d=\gcd(x,y,z)>1
, then
(x/d,y/d,z/d)
is also a solution with
\gcd(x/d,y/d,z/d)=1
. We can further assume that
x,y,z
are pairwise coprime. If
x
and
y
share a common factor
p
, then the left-hand side of (
3
) is divisible by
p^{4}
, and therefore the right-hand side must also be divisible by
p^{4}
, and
n
is not divisible by a fourth power, so
z
must be. A similar argument shows that
\gcd(x,z)=\gcd(y,z)=1
.
Therefore, either
x
and
y
are both odd, or
x
and
y
have different parities.

We first consider the case that
x
and
y
are both odd. We use the change of variables
t=(x+y)/2
,
s=(x-y)/2
, where
t
and
s
have different parity and are coprime. After substituting into (
3
), we obtain

8st(s^{2}+t^{2})=nz^{4}.

We must have either that
n
is divisible by
8
, in which case let
n^{\prime}=n/8
and
z=r
, otherwise,
z
is even so can be written as
z=2r
for integer
r
and let
n^{\prime}=2n
. In either case, we obtain

(7)

st(s^{2}+t^{2})=n^{\prime}r^{4}.

Next we consider the case that
x
and
y
have different parity. We use the change of variables
t=x+y
and
s=x-y
, where
s
and
t
are both odd and coprime (because
x
and
y
are coprime). After substituting into (
3
), we obtain

st(s^{2}+t^{2})=2nz^{4},

letting
n^{\prime}=2n
and
z=r
, we obtain (
7
).

Because
s
and
t
are coprime,
s^{2}+t^{2}
is also coprime to
s
and
t
. Solutions to (
7
) must satisfy

(8)

s=au^{4},\quad t=bv^{4},\quad s^{2}+t^{2}=cw^{4},

where
\gcd(au,bv)=\gcd(au,cw)=\gcd(bv,cw)=1
and
abc=n^{\prime}
.
After substituting (
8
) into (
7
), we obtain

a^{2}u^{8}+b^{2}v^{8}=cw^{4}.

∎

If
c
is a perfect fourth power and
a
or
b
is a perfect square, the equation (
5
) is easy to solve as we can reduce the equation to

(9)

X^{4}-Y^{4}=Z^{2},

which only has integer solutions with
XYZ=0
(see Mordell
[
8
, Theorem 2, page 17]
).

2.2.
Local obstructions and Mordell-Weil sieve

In many cases, the equation (
5
) has local obstructions. That is, for some prime
p
and positive integer
k
, all solutions to the equation modulo
p^{k}
have at least two of
u,v,w
divisible by
p
, which contradicts the pairwise coprimality of
u,v,w
.

For other cases, we can implement a similar method as in Section
2.1
, reducing the equations to elliptic curves.
The problem of solving (
5
) can be reduced to finding rational points on the curves

(10)

(i)\,\,Y^{2}=X^{3}+a^{2}b^{2}c^{2}X,\quad(ii)\,\,Y^{2}=X^{3}-a^{2}b^{4}cX,\quad(iii)\,\,Y^{2}=X^{3}-a^{4}b^{2}cX.

We have that for any nontrivial integer solution
(u,v,w)
to (
5
):

(X,Y)=\left(\dfrac{b^{2}cv^{4}}{u^{4}},\dfrac{b^{2}c^{2}v^{2}w^{2}}{u^{6}}\right)
is a rational solution to equation (
10
)(i),

(X,Y)=\left(\dfrac{b^{2}cw^{2}}{u^{4}},\dfrac{b^{4}cv^{4}w}{u^{6}}\right)
is a rational solution to equation (
10
)(ii),

(X,Y)=\left(-\dfrac{a^{2}b^{2}v^{4}}{w^{2}},\dfrac{a^{4}b^{2}u^{4}v^{2}}{w^{3}}\right)
is a rational solution to equation (
10
)(iii).

If any of the curves in (
10
) has rank
0
, then the curve has finitely many rational points, we can use Magma
[
1
]
to find them and to check whether these points correspond to integer solutions
(x,y,z)
with
xyz\neq 0
to (
3
). However, in cases when all of these curves have positive rank, we must use different methods to solve (
5
).

The next method we use is the Mordell-Weil sieve.
The elliptic curves in (
10
) are equivalent to the curves

(11)

(i)\,\,\mathbf{Y}^{2}\mathbf{Z}=\mathbf{X}^{3}+a^{2}b^{2}c^{2}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}^{2},\quad(ii)\,\,\mathbf{Y}^{2}\mathbf{Z}=\mathbf{X}^{3}-a^{2}b^{4}c\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}^{2},\quad(iii)\,\,\mathbf{Y}^{2}\mathbf{Z}=\mathbf{X}^{3}-a^{4}b^{2}c\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}^{2}

which are the curves in homogeneous form. To transform curves (
10
) to curves (
11
) we use the change of variables
(X,Y)\to(\mathbf{X/Z},\mathbf{Y/Z})
. Any rational point
(X,Y)
on (
10
) can be expressed as an integer point
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
on (
11
). For an elliptic curve in (
11
) with positive rank, its rational points are generated by
a finite list of generators
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})=(X_{i}:Y_{i}:Z_{i})
.
We first present Theorem 6.13 of
[
7
]
. Let
\psi
be the “reduction modulo
p

map”, sending any
(X:Y:Z)
to
(X\text{\, mod\,\,}p:Y\text{\, mod\,\,}p:Z\text{\, mod\,\,}p)
.

Theorem 3
.

Let
A,B
be integers such that
D=4A^{3}+27B^{2}\neq 0
. Let
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
be rational points on the elliptic curve

(12)

Y^{2}Z=X^{3}+AXZ^{2}+BZ^{3}.

Let
p
be a prime not dividing
D
. Then

\psi(P_{1}+P_{2})=\psi(P_{1})+\psi(P_{2}),

where the “
+
” on the left-hand side is the usual addition of points on elliptic curves, while the “
+
” on the right-hand side is defined by the same formulas but with arithmetic operations modulo
p
.

The proof of Theorem
3
can be found in
[
12
, Section A.5]
. All rational points on (
12
) are given by

P=\sum_{i=1}^{m}k_{i}P_{i},

where
\{P_{1},\dots,P_{m}\}
is a list of generators. By Theorem
3
,

\psi(P)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}k_{i}\psi(P_{i}).

The sequence
(n\psi(P_{i}))_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
must be periodic with some period
T_{i}
. Hence, we can assume that
0\leq k_{i}<T_{i}
for all
i
. This allows us to compute all possible values of
\psi(P)
. Therefore, although the multiplication of the points
P_{i}
generates infinitely many integer triples
(X_{i}:Y_{i}:Z_{i})
, by taking these triples modulo some prime
p
, which does not
divide
2abc
, we obtain a list of generators of a certain finite abelian group.

Computational implementation.

Fix a prime
p
not dividing
2abc
. We can solve equation (
5
) modulo
p
. For each solution
(u,v,w)
, we can perform the change of variables
(u,v,w)\to(X,Y,Z)
(to reduce the equation to an elliptic curve of the form (
11
)) and obtain a list of possible integer points
(X,Y,Z)
on the curve. We can compute the generators of the curve (
11
) and obtain the group of solutions
(X,Y,Z)
modulo
p
. If the intersection of both lists obtained is empty then there are no solutions to (
5
). For some equations, we may need to execute this procedure on more than one of the curves (
10
) to obtain an empty intersection.

Example 1
.

Let us consider
n=31
. In this case, all triples
(a,b,c)
are in the set

\{(1,1,62),(2,1,31),(31,1,2),(62,1,1),(2,31,1)\}.

For triple
(1,1,62)
, equation (
5
) has no solutions in coprime integers as modulo
8
analysis implies that at least two of the variables are divisible by
2
. For triple
(2,1,31)
, equation (
5
) has no solutions in coprime integers as modulo
4
analysis implies that at least two of the variables are divisible by
2
. For triple
(62,1,1)
, equation (
10
) (iii) has rank
0
. For triple
(2,31,1)
, equation (
10
) (ii) has rank
0
. It remains to consider triple
(31,1,2)
, that is, we need to solve equation

(13)

961u^{8}+v^{8}=2w^{4}

in pairwise coprime variables
u,v,w
. As explained above, we can reduce the equation to the following elliptic curve

(14)

Y^{2}Z=X^{3}+3844XZ^{2}

with the change of variables
(X,Y,Z)=\left(2u^{2}v^{4},4v^{2}w^{2},u^{6}\right)
.

The group of rational points on curve (
14
) is generated by the rational points

\{(98:-1148:1),(0:0:1)\}.

The solutions of (
13
) modulo
5
are

\displaystyle(u,v,w)\in\{

\displaystyle(0,0,0),(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}):n_{i}\in\{1,2,3,4\}\},

after transforming the solutions to variables
X,Y,Z
modulo
5
, we obtain that the rational points on (
14
) modulo
5
are

\displaystyle(X,Y,Z)\in\{

\displaystyle(0,0,0),(2,1,1),(2,4,1)\}.

Multiplying the generators, we obtain that the list of all possible points on (
14
) modulo
5
is

(X,Y,Z)\in\{(0,1,0),(0,0,1),(3,2,1),(3,3,1)\}.

The intersection of these lists is empty. Therefore, there are no integer solutions of (
13
) in pairwise coprime variables.

2.3.
Pythagorean Triples

In many cases, the methods of Section
2.1
and
2.2
are enough to solve (
3
) completely. We next give an elementary method which can be implemented if
c
is a perfect square.

It is well known that all integer solutions to the equation

(15)

x^{2}+y^{2}=z^{2}

can be described as a finite union of polynomial parametrisations and we call solutions
(x,y,z)
Pythagorean triples.
Moreover, any solution
(x,y,z)
to (
15
) with
x,y,z
pairwise coprime is called a primitive Pythagorean triple and it is well known
[
7
]
that these solutions (up to the exchange of
x
and
y
) are completely described by

(16)

(x,y,z)=(2mn,m^{2}-n^{2},m^{2}+n^{2}),\quad m,n\in\mathbb{Z},\quad 2|mn\text{ and }\gcd(m,n)=1.

By (
5
),
a
and
b
are either opposite parity or they are both odd. If
a
and
b
have opposite parity, we label the equation so that
a
is even.
Given an equation (
5
) with
c
a perfect square, that is
c=k^{2}
for some positive integer
k
. We can then reduce (
5
) to (
15
) using the change of variables
x=au^{4}
,
y=bv^{4}
and
z=kw^{2}
. We then obtain the system of equations

(17)

2mn=au^{4},\quad m^{2}-n^{2}=bv^{4},\quad m^{2}+n^{2}=kw^{2}.

If
a
and
b
are both odd, then only one of
u
or
v
may be even, so we must also solve the system

(18)

m^{2}-n^{2}=au^{4},\quad 2mn=bv^{4},\quad m^{2}+n^{2}=kw^{2}.

As
a,b,k
are positive, we may assume that
m>n>0
. Then equation
2mn=au^{4}
has two-monomials and for which we can describe all solutions with
m,n
coprime, and these will be of the form
m=dU^{4}
,
n=eV^{4}
where
d,e
are coprime integers and
U,V
are coprime variables. We then substitute these into
m^{2}-n^{2}=bv^{4}
and obtain
2
2

2

The equation of the form (
19
) for (
18
) is obtained similarly.

(19)

d^{2}U^{8}-e^{2}V^{8}=bv^{4},

and we inherit the condition

(20)

\gcd(dU,eV)=\gcd(dU,bv)=\gcd(eV,bv)=1

which is a similar equation to (
5
) and we can use the same methods described earlier. Note that in this case, the curves (
10
) are now

(i)\,\,Y^{2}=X^{3}-d^{2}e^{2}b^{2}X,\quad(ii)\,\,Y^{2}=X^{3}-d^{2}e^{4}bX,\quad(iii)\,\,Y^{2}=X^{3}+d^{4}e^{2}bX.

Example 2
.

To illustrate this method, we consider (
3
) with
n=885
. The methods of Sections
2.1
and
2.2
cannot solve the equation

(21)

36u^{8}+87025v^{8}=w^{4},

which is (
5
) with
(a,b,c)=(6,295,1)
.
We can then reduce (
21
) to (
15
) using the change of variables
x=6u^{4}
,
y=295v^{4}
and
z=w^{2}
. By (
16
), we then obtain the system of equations

2mn=6u^{4},\quad m^{2}-n^{2}=295v^{4},\quad m^{2}+n^{2}=w^{2}.

We have
mn=3u^{4}
, whose solutions in coprime variables are
(m,n,u)=(3U^{4},V^{4},UV)
or
(U^{4},3V^{4},UV)
for coprime integers
U,V
. In the first case, substituting
(m,n)=(3U^{4},V^{4})
into
m^{2}-n^{2}=295v^{4}
we obtain

9U^{8}-V^{8}=295v^{4}

and modulo
3
analysis shows that
V
and
v
are both divisible by
3
, however then both
m
and
n
are divisible by
3
, a contradiction.
In the second case, substituting
(m,n)=(U^{4},3V^{4})
into
m^{2}-n^{2}=295v^{4}
we obtain

U^{8}-9V^{8}=295v^{4}

and modulo
5
analysis shows that this equation has no solutions with coprime
U,V
.
Therefore (
21
) has no integer solutions satisfying (
6
) and this completes the proof that (
3
) with
n=885
has no integer solutions with
xyz\neq 0
.

2.4.
Implementation for
1\leq n\leq 10000

We implemented the methods of Sections
2.1
and
2.2
in Magma
[
1
]
. The code first checks whether the equation has a nonzero integer solution. It next calculates the rank of the curves (
4
) and excludes equations which have rank
0
, it finds all triples
(a,b,c)
satisfying
abc=n^{\prime}
, checks for local solubility, and then proceeds to calculate rank of curves (
10
). If the rank of all the curves are positive, it tries to find a suitable prime to obtain a contradiction in the group of rational points. If it cannot find a suitable prime
p<229
it outputs the unsolvable case, which needs to be investigated by hand as to whether it can be solved using the method of Section
3
. Continuing the investigation up to
n=10000
, the code outputs the equations in Table
1
as not solvable by methods of Sections
2.1
,
2.2
. As we can see from Table
2
, the methods of Sections
2.1
,
2.2
, and
2.3
can determine the solubility of all equations (
3
) with
1\leq n\leq 218
, and the only exceptions in the range
1\leq n\leq 1000
are
n=219,463
, and
514
. Overall, there are
33
(non-equivalent) values of
n
in the range
1\leq n\leq 10000
for which these methods are not sufficient. Equation (
3
) with
n=219
is solved with details in
[
14
]
. We will see in Section
3
that all other remaining cases in the range
1\leq n\leq 10000
are also solvable by the method in
[
14
]
.

2.5.
How to use the Magma program

Load
3
3

3

The Magma files for this implementation are available on Github
https://github.com/ashleighratcliffe/DifferenceOfPowers
.
file “diffpowerex.m” (which also loads files “fullsieve.m” and “sieve.m”), the program is executed using the command

\texttt{Diff4powers(k,m)},

where
k
and
m
are integers which are the coefficients of the equation

(22)

kx^{4}-ky^{4}=mz^{4}.

The outputs can be “factor 4th power”, “excluded at easy curves”, “Exclude by Mordell’s Theorem”,
[\,\,]
, or a set of equations. The first means the integer is equivalent to solving one with smaller
n
4
4

4

Because we did this investigation in order of
n
, the equation with smaller
n
have already been solved.
. The second means that at least one of the curves (
4
) has rank
0
. The third means that some equations (
5
) are reducible to solving (
9
). The fourth means that all triples
(a,b,c)
have corresponding equations (
5
) have either local obstructions or at least one of the corresponding curves (
10
) have rank
0
, or the equation is solvable using the Mordell-Weil sieve method explained above. If the output is a set of equations, these equations must be solved using the method in Section
2.3
or
3
.

.

. This method works in all but 6 cases. The details to solve these equations are presented in Table

. This significantly reduces the number of equations left to solve, and this list is presented in Table
2
, and Section
3
solves these equations explicitly.

or

. Solving the equations in Table
2
is the subject of Section
3
.

3.
Factorization in
{\mathbb{Z}}[i]

In this section, we use the method from
[
14
]
to show that none of the equations listed in Table
5
5

5

In this section we choose to solve all equations in Table
1
to show details for solving all equations not excluded by the diffpowers program.

1
has integer solutions
(u,v,w)
satisfying (
6
). This will complete the proof of Theorem
1
.
The main idea of this method is as follows. First, we write equation (
5
) as

(au^{4}+bv^{4}i)(au^{4}-bv^{4}i)=cw^{4}.

Then there exist integers
s,t
, and a Gaussian integer
\alpha|c
such that

(23)

au^{4}+bv^{4}i=\alpha(s+ti)^{4}.

Equating the real and imaginary parts on both sides of (
23
), we obtain a system

(24)

\begin{cases}u^{4}=F_{\alpha}(s,t),\\
v^{4}=G_{\alpha}(s,t),\end{cases}

where
F_{\alpha}(s,t)
and
G_{\alpha}(s,t)
are degree four homogeneous polynomials in
{\mathbb{Z}}[s,t]
. System (
24
) defines a scheme in the projective space
\mathbb{P}^{3}({\mathbb{Q}})
. Using Magma
[
1
]
, one can check that this scheme is locally insoluble at a suitable prime.

We now implement this method to solve all the remaining equations in Table
2
. See Appendix
B
.

4.
Conclusion

In summary, the methods of this paper allow us to solve all equations (
3
) with
1\leq n\leq 10000
in integers with
xyz\neq 0
. Table
3
lists all integers
1\leq n\leq 10000
that can be expressed as a difference of two rational fourth powers, and such a representation. The lists of representable integers are available on the On-line Encyclopedia of Integer Sequences (OEIS)
[
10
]
, specifically, the positive integers expressible as a difference of two nonzero rational fourth powers is
A393616
and the positive integers expressible as a difference of two rational fourth powers is
A393617
. For all the equations which are outputted by the Magma program described in Section
2.5
, the necessary information and computations to prove the non-existence of integer solutions to these equations are given in the appendices of the extended version of this paper at
http://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15832
.

) (or respectively

) by solving the equation modulo

.

B.2.
The case of
n=463
.

According to Table
2
, in the case
n=463
it remains to
show that equation

(25)

214369u^{8}+v^{8}=2w^{4}.

has no integer solutions satisfying (
6
), which in this case reduces to

\gcd(463u,v)=\gcd(u,2w)=\gcd(v,2w)=1.

Hence,
2\nmid uv
.
Write (
25
) as

(26)

(463u^{4}+v^{4}i)(463u^{4}-v^{4}i)=(1+i)(1-i)w^{4}.

Let
d\in{\mathbb{Z}}[i]
such that
d|\gcd(463u^{4}+v^{4}i,463u^{4}-v^{4}i)
. Then
d|926u^{4}
,
d|2v^{4}
,
d|2w^{4}
. Hence,
d|2
. Note that
u
and
v
are odd.
This implies that there exist integers
s,t
such that

463u^{4}+v^{4}i=i^{\epsilon}(1+i)(s+ti)^{4},

with
\epsilon\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.

B.4.

.

According to Table
2
, in the case
n=1305
it remains to
show that equation

(41)

81u^{8}+4v^{8}=145w^{4}

has no integer solutions satisfying (

), which in this case reduces to

\gcd(3u,2v)=\gcd(2u,145w)=\gcd(2v,145w)=1,

and to show that equation

has no integer solutions satisfying (

), which in this case reduces to

\gcd(18u,v)=\gcd(18u,145w)=\gcd(v,145w)=1.

B.4.1.
The case of (
41
).

We first consider (
41
), and we can write this as

(43)

(9u^{4}+2v^{4}i)(9u^{4}-2iv^{4})=(1+2i)(1-2i)(5+2i)(5-2i)w^{4}.

Hence,
\gcd(9u^{4}+2v^{4}i,9u^{4}-2v^{4}i)=1
and

9u^{4}+2v^{4}i\equiv 2i-1\pmod{5}.

Thus
1-2i|9u^{4}+2v^{4}i
. This implies that there exist integers
s,t
such that

9u^{4}+2v^{4}i=i^{\epsilon}(1-2i)(5\pm 2i)(s+ti)^{4},

\epsilon\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.

B.4.2.
The case of (
42
).

We now consider (
42
), which we can write as

(52)

(18u^{4}+v^{4}i)(18u^{4}-iv^{4})=(1+2i)(1-2i)(5+2i)(5-2i)w^{4}.

Hence,

\begin{split}18u^{4}+iv^{4}&\equiv-2+i\pmod{5}\\
&\equiv 0\pmod{1+2i}\\
&\not\equiv 0\pmod{1-2i}.\end{split}

This implies that there exist integers
s,t
such that

18u^{4}+v^{4}i=i^{\epsilon}(1+2i)(5\pm 2i)(s+ti)^{4},

\epsilon\in\{0,1,2,3\}

B.11.
The case of
n=2719
.

According to Table
2
, in the case
n=2719
it remains to
show that equation

(117)

7392961u^{8}+v^{8}=2w^{4}

has no integer solutions satisfying (
6
), which in this case reduces to

(118)

\gcd(2719u,v)=\gcd(2719u,2w)=\gcd(v,2w)=1.

Write (
117
) as

(119)

(2719u^{4}+v^{4}i)(2719u^{4}-v^{4}i)=(1+i)(1-i)w^{4}.

This implies that there exist integers
s,t
such that

2719u^{4}+v^{4}i=i^{\epsilon}(s+ti)^{4},

with
\epsilon\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.

Case 1:

2719u^{4}+v^{4}i=(s+ti)^{4}
. Equating the real and imaginary parts on both sides of this equation gives

(120)

\begin{cases}s^{4}-6s^{2}t^{2}+t^{4}-2719u^{4}=0,\\
4s^{3}t-4st^{3}-v^{4}=0.\end{cases}

The scheme defined by system (
120
) is locally insoluble at
2
.

⬇

P
<
s
,
t
,
u
,
v
>:=
ProjectiveSpace
(
Rationals
(),3);

:=

s
^4

-

6*
s
^2*
t
^2

+

t
^4-2719*
u
^4;

B
:=4*
s
^3*
t

-

4*
s
*
t
^3-
v
^4;

S
:=

(
P
,[
A
,
B
]);

IsLocallySolvable
(
S
,2);

Case 2:

2719u^{4}+v^{4}i=i(s+ti)^{4}
. Equating the real and imaginary parts on both sides of this equation gives

(121)

\begin{cases}-4s^{3}t+4st^{3}-2719u^{4}=0,\\
s^{4}-6s^{2}t^{2}+t^{4}-v^{4}=0.\end{cases}

The equation
X^{4}-6X^{2}Y^{2}+Y^{4}=Z^{2}
has no integer solutions
(X,Y,Z)
with
XY\neq 0
(see Mordell
[
8
, Theorem 3,page 18]
. It follows from this and the second equation in (
121
) that
st=0
. Then the first equation forces
u=0
, which is not allowed.

Case 3:

2719u^{4}+v^{4}i=i^{2}(s+ti)^{4}
. Equating the real and imaginary parts on both sides of this equation gives

(122)

\begin{cases}-s^{4}+6s^{2}t^{2}-t^{4}-2719u^{4}=0,\\
-4s^{3}t+4st^{3}-v^{4}=0\end{cases}

Modulo
2
analysis on the second equation returns that any integer solution must have
v
even, however this is a contradiction with (
118
).

B.13.
The case of
n=3185
.

According to Table
2
, in the case
n=3185
it remains to
show that equation

(134)

2401u^{8}+4v^{8}=65w^{4}

has no integer solutions satisfying (
6
), which in this case reduces to

\gcd(7u,2v)=\gcd(7u,65w)=\gcd(2v,65w)=1,

and to show that equation

(135)

9604u^{8}+v^{8}=65w^{4}

has no integer solutions satisfying (
6
), which in this case reduces to

\gcd(98u,v)=\gcd(98u,65w)=\gcd(v,65w)=1.

, in the case

), which in this case reduces to

) as

(177)

, we have

\begin{split}8u^{4}+v^{4}i&\equiv-2+i\pmod{5}\\

. This implies that there exist integers

and to show that equation

\gcd(98u,v)=\gcd(98u,85w)=\gcd(v,85w)=1.

B.17.1.
The case of (
186
).

We first consider (
186
), which we can write (
186
) as

(189)

(49u^{4}+2v^{4}i)(49u^{4}-2iv^{4})=(2+i)(2-i)(4+i)(4-i)w^{4}.

By (
187
) we have that
5\nmid uv
, hence
u^{4}\equiv v^{4}\equiv 1
(mod
5
). Therefore,

\begin{cases}49u^{4}+2v^{4}i&\equiv-1+2i\pmod{5}\\
&\equiv 0\pmod{2+i}\\
&\not\equiv 0\pmod{2-i}.\end{cases}.

Let
d\in{\mathbb{Z}}[i]
such that
d|\gcd(49u^{4}+2v^{4}i,49u^{4}-2v^{4}i)
. Then
d|2\cdot 49u^{4}
,
d|4v^{4}
,
d|85w^{4}
. Combining this with (
187
) shows that
d|1
. This implies that there exist integers
s,t
such that

49u^{4}+2v^{4}i=i^{\epsilon}(2+i)(4\pm i)(s+ti)^{4},

with
\epsilon\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.

B.17.2.
The case of (
188
).

We now consider (
188
), which we can write (
188
) as

(198)

(98u^{4}+v^{4}i)(98u^{4}-v^{4}i)=(2+i)(2-i)(4+i)(4-i)w^{4}.

Since
5\nmid u,v
, we have

\begin{split}98u^{4}+v^{4}i&\equiv-2+i\pmod{5}\\
&\equiv 0\pmod{2-i}\\
&\not\equiv 0\pmod{2+i}.\end{split}

Hence,
2-i|98u^{4}+v^{4}i
. This implies that there exist integers
s,t
such that

98u^{4}+v^{4}i=i^{\epsilon}(2-i)(4\pm i)(s+ti)^{4},

\epsilon\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.

B.19.
The case of
n=4901
.

According to Table
2
, in the case
n=4901
it remains to
show that equation

(217)

4u^{8}+v^{8}=4901w^{4}

has no integer solutions satisfying (
6
), which in this case reduces to

\gcd(2u,v)=\gcd(2u,4901w)=\gcd(v,4901w)=1.

Write (
217
) as

(218)

(2u^{4}+v^{4}i)(2u^{4}-v^{4}i)=(3+2i)^{2}(3-2i)^{2}(5+2i)(5-2i)w^{4}.

This implies that there exist integers
s,t
such that

2u^{4}+v^{4}i=i^{\epsilon}(3\pm 2i)^{2}(5\pm 2i)(s+ti)^{4},

with
\epsilon\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.

B.20.
The case of
n=4961
.

According to Table
2
, in the case
n=4961
it remains to
show that equation

(235)

14641u^{8}+v^{8}=82w^{4}

has no integer solutions satisfying (
6
), which in this case reduces to

\gcd(121u,v)=\gcd(121u,82w)=\gcd(v,82w)=1.

Write (
235
) as

(236)

(121u^{4}+v^{4}i)(121u^{4}-v^{4}i)=(1+i)(1-i)(5+4i)(5-4i)w^{4}.

This implies that there exist integers

) we must have
2\nmid uw
. We can write (

B.25.1.
The case of (
274
).

We first consider (
274
), which we can write as

(276)

(2u^{4}+v^{4}i)(2u^{4}-v^{4}i)=(2+i)^{3}(2-i)^{3}(7+2i)(7-2i)w^{4}.

Then,

\begin{split}2u^{4}+v^{4}i&\equiv 2+i\pmod{5}\\
&\equiv 0\pmod{2+i}\\
&\not\equiv 0\pmod{2-i}.\end{split}

s,t

2u^{4}+v^{4}i=i^{\epsilon}(2+i)^{3}(7\pm 2i)(s+ti)^{4},

B.32.
The case of
n=8931
.

According to Table
2
, in the case
n=8931
it remains to
show that equation

(346)

36u^{8}+v^{8}=2977w^{4}

has no integer solutions satisfying (
6
), which in this case reduces to

\gcd(6u,v)=\gcd(6u,2977w)=\gcd(v,2977w)=1.

Write (
346
) as

(347)

(6u^{4}+v^{4}i)(6u^{4}-v^{4}i)=(3+2i)(3-2i)(15+2i)(15-2i)w^{4}.

This implies that there exist integers
s,t
such that

6u^{4}+v^{4}i=i^{\epsilon}(3\pm 2i)(15\pm 2i)(s+ti)^{4},

with
\epsilon\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.

B.33.
The case of
n=9015
.

According to Table
2
, in the case
n=9015
it remains to
show that equation

(364)

36u^{8}+25v^{8}=601w^{4}

has no integer solutions satisfying (
6
), which in this case reduces to

\gcd(6u,5v)=\gcd(6u,601w)=\gcd(5v,601w)=1.

Write (
364
) as

(365)

(6u^{4}+5v^{4}i)(6u^{4}-5v^{4}i)=(24+5i)(24-5i)w^{4}.

This implies that there exist integers
s,t
such that

6u^{4}+5v^{4}i=i^{\epsilon}(24\pm 5i)(s+ti)^{4},

with
\epsilon\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.

Case 1:

6u^{4}+5v^{4}i=(24+5i)(s+ti)^{4}
. Equating the real and imaginary parts on both sides of this equation gives

(366)

\begin{cases}24s^{4}-20s^{3}t-144s^{2}t^{2}+20st^{3}+24t^{4}-6u^{4}=0,\\
5s^{4}+96s^{3}t-30s^{2}t^{2}-96st^{3}+5t^{4}-5v^{4}=0.\end{cases}

The scheme defined by system (
366
) is locally insoluble at
2
.

⬇

,

,

,
